The '''2014 United States Senate elections''' were held on November 4, 2014. A total of 36 seats in the 100-member [[United States Senate|U.S. Senate]] were contested. There were 33 [[Classes of United States senators#Class 2|Class 2]] seats contested for regular six-year terms to be served from January 3, 2015, to January 3, 2021, and three [[Classes of United States senators#Class 3|Class 3]] seats were contested in [[Special elections to the United States Senate|special elections]] due to Senate vacancies. The elections marked 100 years of [[Seventeenth Amendment to the United States Constitution|direct elections of U.S. senators]]. Going into the elections, 21 of the contested seats were held by the Democratic Party, while 15 were held by the Republican Party.

The Republicans regained the majority of the Senate in the [[114th United States Congress|114th Congress]], which started in January 2015; the Republicans had not controlled the Senate since [[2006 United States Senate elections|January 2007]]. They needed a net gain of at least six seats to obtain a majority and were projected by polls to do so. On election night, they held all of their seats and gained nine Democratic-held seats. Republicans defeated five Democratic incumbents: [[Mark Begich]] of Alaska lost to [[Dan Sullivan (U.S. senator)|Dan Sullivan]], [[Mark Pryor]] of Arkansas lost to [[Tom Cotton]], [[Mark Udall]] of Colorado lost to [[Cory Gardner]], [[Mary Landrieu]] of Louisiana lost to [[Bill Cassidy]], and [[Kay Hagan]] of North Carolina lost to [[Thom Tillis]]. Republicans also picked up another 4 open seats in [[2014 United States Senate election in Iowa|Iowa]], [[2014 United States Senate election in Montana|Montana]], [[2014 United States Senate election in South Dakota|South Dakota]], and [[2014 United States Senate election in West Virginia|West Virginia]] that were previously held by Democrats. Democrats did not pick up any Republican-held seats, but they did hold an open seat in [[2014 United States Senate election in Michigan|Michigan]].

This was the most recent time that any party lost control of the Senate in a midterm election cycle. With a net gain of nine seats, the Republicans made the largest Senate gain by any party since [[1980 United States Senate elections|1980]]. This is also the first election cycle since 1980 in which more than two [[incumbent]] Democratic senators were defeated by their Republican challengers.<ref>{{cite web |last1=Klein |first1=Ezra |title=Democrats sat out the 2014 midterms and lost the Supreme Court for a generation |url=https://www.vox.com/policy-and-politics/2018/6/26/17506054/anthony-kennedy-retirement-supreme-court |website=Vox |date=June 26, 2018 |publisher=Vox Media, LLC |access-date=18 May 2021}}</ref> Days after the election cycle, the United States Election Project estimated that 36.4% of eligible voters voted, 4% lower than the 2010 elections, and possibly the lowest turnout rate since the [[1942 United States elections|1942 election cycle]].<ref name="LCook">{{Cite news |last=Cook |first=Lindsay |date=November 5, 2014 |title=Midterm Turnout Down in 2014 |publisher=U.S. News & World Report |url=https://www.usnews.com/news/blogs/data-mine/2014/11/05/midterm-turnout-decreased-in-all-but-12-states |access-date=November 7, 2014}}</ref><ref name="USAVT">{{Cite news |last=Camia |first=Catalina |date=November 6, 2014 |title=Voter turnout could be lowest since World War II |publisher=USA Today |url=http://onpolitics.usatoday.com/2014/11/06/voter-turnout-2014-elections |access-date=November 7, 2014}}</ref>

This remains the most recent time that a Republican has won a U.S. Senate election in [[2014 United States Senate election in Colorado|Colorado]], that the president's party has suffered a net loss of Senate seats in a midterm election cycle, and that an incumbent senator was reelected unopposed.

==Partisan composition==
For a majority, Republicans needed at least 51 seats. Democrats could have retained a majority with 48 seats (assuming the two Independents continued to caucus with them) because the Democratic Vice President [[Joe Biden]] would become the tie-breaker. From 1915 to 2013, control of the U.S. Senate flipped in 10 of 50 cycles, or 20% of the time.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Ostermeier |first=Eric |date=October 20, 2014 |title=Why Are We Obsessed with the 2014 US Senate Elections? |url=http://editions.lib.umn.edu/smartpolitics/2014/10/20/why-are-we-obsessed-with-the-2 |website=Smart Politics}}</ref> [[Republican Party (United States)|Republicans]] had lost ground in [[2012 United States Senate elections|the 2012 elections]], leading to an internal fight among the [[Republican Party (United States)|Republican]] leadership over the best strategies and tactics for the 2014 Senate races.<ref>{{Cite news |last=Zeleny |first=Jeff |date=February 6, 2013 |title=New Rove Effort Has G.O.P. Aflame |work=[[The New York Times]] |url=https://www.nytimes.com/2013/02/07/us/politics/new-rove-effort-has-gop-aflame.html?_r=1& |access-date=February 7, 2013}}</ref> By December 2013, eight of the twelve incumbent Republicans running for re-election saw [[Tea Party movement|Tea Party]] challenges.<ref name="Tea Party primaries">{{Cite news |last=Raju |first=Manu |date=December 10, 2013 |title=Tea partiers line up to tackle GOP senators |work=Politico |url=https://www.politico.com/story/2013/12/tea-party-gop-senators-100988.html |access-date=December 16, 2013}}</ref> However, Republican incumbents won every primary challenge.<ref name="CivilWar">{{Cite news |last=Palmer |first=Anna |date=August 8, 2014 |title=GOP civil war to rage on |publisher=Politico |url=https://www.politico.com/story/2014/08/2014-elections-republican-unity-109830.html?hp=l2 |access-date=August 8, 2014}}</ref> Although Democrats saw some opportunities for pickups, the combination of Democratic retirements and numerous Democratic seats up for election in [[swing state]]s and [[Red states and blue states|red state]]s gave Republicans hopes of taking control of the Senate.<ref name="Fix December">{{Cite news |last=Sullivan |first=Sean |date=December 6, 2013 |title=The Fix's top 10 Senate races of 2014 |newspaper=[[The Washington Post]] |url=https://www.washingtonpost.com/blogs/the-fix/wp/2013/12/06/the-fixs-top-10-senate-races-of-2014-6 |access-date=November 4, 2014}}</ref> 7 of the 21 states with Democratic seats up for election in 2014 had voted for Republican [[Mitt Romney]] in the [[United States Presidential Election, 2012|2012 presidential election]] compared to just 1 state with a Republican senator that voted for Barack Obama. Democrats also faced the lower [[voter turnout]] that accompanies [[Midterm election|midterm elections]].<ref name="PewMT">{{Cite news |last=DeSilver |first=Drew |date=July 24, 2014 |title=Voter turnout always drops off for midterm elections, but why? |publisher=Pew |url=http://www.pewresearch.org/fact-tank/2014/07/24/voter-turnout-always-drops-off-for-midterm-elections-but-why |access-date=November 7, 2014}}</ref>

[[Poll aggregation]] website [[FiveThirtyEight]] gave the Republican Party a 60% chance of taking control of the Senate as of September 28.<ref>{{Cite web |date=September 16, 2014 |title=Senate Forecast |url=https://fivethirtyeight.com/interactives/senate-forecast/ |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20140906132925/http://fivethirtyeight.com/interactives/senate-forecast/ |url-status=dead |archive-date=September 6, 2014 |access-date=September 16, 2014 |website=[[FiveThirtyEight]]}}</ref> Another poll aggregation website, [[RealClearPolitics]], gave the Republican Party a net gain of 7 seats.<ref>{{Cite web |date=September 16, 2014 |title=Senate no toss ups |url=http://www.realclearpolitics.com/epolls/2014/senate/2014_elections_senate_map_no_toss_ups.html |access-date=September 16, 2014 |website=RealClearPolitics}}</ref> Due to the closeness of several races, it was initially believed that Senate control might not be decided on election night.<ref name=SDavis/> Both Louisiana and Georgia were seen as competitive, and both states required a [[runoff election]] if no candidate won a majority of the vote.

Two [[independent (United States)|independent]] candidates (in Kansas and South Dakota<ref name="ABlake">{{Cite news |last=Blake |first=Aaron |date=October 8, 2014 |title=There's something very interesting happening in South Dakota |newspaper=[[The Washington Post]] |url=https://www.washingtonpost.com/news/the-fix/wp/2014/10/08/theres-something-very-interesting-happening-in-south-dakota/ |access-date=October 9, 2014}}</ref>) refused to commit to caucusing with either party.<ref name="SDavis">{{Cite news |last=Davis |first=Susan |date=October 7, 2014 |title=Senate control may be undecided for weeks after election |publisher=USA Today |url=https://www.usatoday.com/story/news/politics/2014/10/07/senate-control-delay/16858721 |access-date=October 9, 2014}}</ref> In the final months of the race, polls showed them with viable chances of winning, leading some analysts to speculate on the possibility of an "Independent caucus" that could also include Maine Senator [[Angus King]] and possibly Vermont Senator [[Bernie Sanders]].<ref name="Indywave">{{Cite news |last=Jaffe |first=Alexandra |date=October 11, 2014 |title=Independents — wave of future? |work=The Hill |url=https://thehill.com/blogs/ballot-box/220477-independents-wave-of-the-future/ |access-date=October 13, 2014}}</ref><ref name="LKillian">{{Cite news |last=Killian |first=Linda |date=October 13, 2014 |title=The Independents Who Could Tip the Senate in November |publisher=The Daily Beast |url=http://www.thedailybeast.com/articles/2014/10/13/the-independents-who-could-tip-the-senate-in-november.html |access-date=October 13, 2014}}</ref> However, no Independent won a Senate race in 2014, and King and Sanders continue to caucus with the Democratic Party following the 2014 election cycle.

By midnight [[Eastern Time Zone|ET]], most major networks projected that the Republicans would take control of the Senate. The party held all three competitive Republican-held seats (Kentucky, Kansas, and Georgia), and defeated incumbent Democrats in North Carolina, Colorado, and Arkansas. Combined with the pick-ups of open seats in Iowa, Montana, South Dakota, and West Virginia, the Republicans made a net gain of 7 seats before the end of the night. Republicans defeated three incumbent Democrats, a task the party had not accomplished since the [[1980 United States Senate elections|1980 election cycle]].<ref name="DIncumbents">{{Cite news |last=Kondik |first=Kyle |date=July 17, 2014 |title=The Hidden Barrier to a Republican Senate Majority |publisher=Sabato's Crystal Ball |url=http://www.centerforpolitics.org/crystalball/articles/the-hidden-barrier-to-a-republican-senate-majority |access-date=July 18, 2014}}</ref> Five of the seven confirmed pickups were in states that voted for [[Mitt Romney]] in 2012, but two of the seats that Republicans won represent states that voted for [[Barack Obama]] in 2012 (Colorado and Iowa). Of the three races that were not called by the end of election night, Alaska and Virginia were still too close to call, while Louisiana held a December 6 runoff election. Virginia declared incumbent Democrat [[Mark Warner]] the winner of his race by a narrow margin over Republican [[Ed Gillespie]] on November 7, and Alaska declared [[Dan Sullivan (United States Senator, Alaska)|Dan Sullivan]] the winner against Democratic incumbent [[Mark Begich]] on November 12. Republican [[Bill Cassidy]] defeated Democratic incumbent [[Mary Landrieu]] in the Louisiana runoff on December 6.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Jaffe |first=Alexandra |date=2014-12-07 |title=Mary Landrieu swept away by red tide as GOP picks up 9th seat {{!}} CNN Politics |url=https://www.cnn.com/2014/12/06/politics/louisiana-senate-runoff-results/index.html |access-date=2025-05-09 |website=CNN |language=en}}</ref>

===Election spending===
Altogether, at least $3.67 billion was spent by candidates, parties, committees and outside groups in the 2014 election cycle.<ref>Chris Cillizza, [https://www.washingtonpost.com/news/the-fix/wp/2014/11/06/the-2014-election-cost-3-7-billion-we-spend-twice-that-much-on-halloween/ The 2014 election cost $3.7 billion. We spend twice that much on Halloween.], ''Washington Post'' (November 6, 2014).</ref><ref>[http://www.opensecrets.org/news/2014/10/overall-spending-inches-up-in-2014-megadonors-equip-outside-groups-to-capture-a-bigger-share-of-the-pie/ Overall Spending Inches Up in 2014: Megadonors Equip Outside Groups to Capture a Bigger Share of the Pie], [[OpenSecrets]] (October 29, 2014).</ref> Spending on the 2014 Senate elections by outside groups (i.e., organizations other than a candidate's campaign, such as [[super PACs]] and "[[dark money]]" nonprofit groups) more than doubled from 2010. In the 10 competitive races for which data were available, outside groups accounted for 47% of spending, candidates accounted for 41% of spending, and parties accounted for 12% of spending.<ref name="Vandewalker">Ian Vandewalker, ''[https://www.brennancenter.org/sites/default/files/publications/Outside%20Spending%20Since%20Citizens%20United.pdf Election Spending 2014: Outside Spending in Senate Races Since Citizens United]'', Brennan Center for Justice (2015).</ref> The Senate race with the most outside spending was in North Carolina, at $80 million, a new record.<ref name=Vandewalker/>

The top outside spenders in the 11 most competitive Senate races were the following:

* On the Republican side, the [[National Republican Senatorial Committee]], [[Crossroads GPS]], [[U.S. Chamber of Commerce]], Ending Spending Action Fund, [[Freedom Partners Action Fund]], [[American Crossroads]], and the [[Political Victory Fund|NRA Political Victory Fund]].<ref name=Vandewalker/>
* On the Democratic side, the [[Democratic Senatorial Campaign Committee]], Senate Majority PAC, and [[NextGen Climate Action Committee]].<ref name=Vandewalker/>

==Results summary==
The Republican Party made a net gain of nine U.S. Senate seats in the 2014 elections.<ref>{{Cite web |title=Meet the new senators |url=https://www.usatoday.com/story/news/politics/elections/2014/11/05/new-senators-2014-elections/17781205/ |website=USA TODAY}}</ref>

Going into the elections, there were 53 Democratic, 45 Republican and 2 [[Independent (United States)|independent]] senators (both of whom [[Democratic Caucus of the United States Senate|caucus with the Democrats]]). In all, there were 36 elections: 33 senators were up for election this year as [[Classes of United States senators|class 2 senators]], and 3 faced special elections (all from Class 3). Of all these seats, 21 were held by Democrats and 15 were held by Republicans.

== Gains and losses ==
===Retirements===
[[File:2014 United States Senate elections retirements map.svg|thumb|250px|Map of retirements:<br/>{{legend|#FF9998|Republican incumbent}}
{{legend|#93C5DD|Democratic incumbent}}
{{legend|#0671B0|Democratic incumbent retired or withdrew}}
{{legend|#CA0020|Republican incumbent retired{{efn|name=Oklahoma}}}}
]]
Four Democrats and two Republicans retired instead of seeking re-election. [[Max Baucus]] of [[Montana]] announced his intent to retire at the end of his term, but he resigned on February 6, 2014, after being appointed as the [[United States Ambassador to China]]. [[John Walsh (Montana politician)|John Walsh]], who was appointed by Montana governor [[Steve Bullock (Montana politician)|Steve Bullock]] to finish Baucus's term, later withdrew to run for a full term (see ''Nomination withdrawn'' section below).

==Race summaries==

=== Special elections during the preceding Congress ===

=== Elections leading to the next Congress ===
In these general elections, the winners were elected for the term beginning January 3, 2015; ordered by state.

All of the elections involved the Class 2 seats.

===Predicted probability of Republican takeover===
Several websites used [[poll aggregation]] and [[psephology]] to estimate the probability that the Republican Party would gain enough seats to take control of the Senate.

===Predictions===
Republicans needed to win at least six in order to gain a majority of 51 seats and Democrats needed to win at least seven in order to hold a majority of 50 seats (including the two independents who currently caucus with the Democrats) and the [[Vice President of the United States#President of the Senate|tie-breaking vote]] of [[Vice President of the United States|Vice President]] [[Joe Biden]].

{{Main|2014 United States Senate election in Alabama}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Alabama}}

Three-term incumbent Republican [[Jeff Sessions]] had been re-elected with 63% of the vote in 2008. Sessions sought re-election. Democrat Victor Sanchez Williams ran against Sessions as a write-in candidate.<ref>{{Cite web |title=VICTOR SANCHEZ |url=https://www.fec.gov/data/candidate/S4AL00091/WILLIAMS, |access-date=December 21, 2017}}{{Dead link|date=November 2018 |bot=InternetArchiveBot |fix-attempted=yes }}</ref> Sessions won with 97.3 percent of the vote<ref>{{Cite web |title=2014 United States Senate election in Alabama |url=http://ballotpedia.org/United_States_Senate_elections_in_Alabama,_2014 |access-date=April 27, 2015 |website=Ballotpedia}}</ref> against assorted write-in candidates.<ref>{{Cite web |title=State of Alabama Canvass of Results: General Election, November 4, 2014 |url=http://www.alabamavotes.gov/downloads/election/2014/general/2014GeneralResults-WithWriteIn.pdf |access-date=April 27, 2015 |website=Alabama Votes |publisher=Secretary of the State of Alabama}}</ref>

== Alaska ==
{{Infobox election
 | election_name      = Alaska election
 | country            = Alaska
 | type               = presidential
 | ongoing            = no
 | previous_election  = 2008 United States Senate election in Alaska
 | previous_year      = 2008
 | next_election      = 2020 United States Senate election in Alaska
 | next_year          = 2020
 | image_size         = 125x136px

 | image1             = Senator Dan Sullivan official.jpg
 | nominee1           = '''[[Dan Sullivan (U.S. Senator)|Dan Sullivan]]'''
 | party1             = Republican Party (US)
 | popular_vote1      = '''135,445'''
 | percentage1        = '''48.0%'''

 | image2             = Mark Begich, official portrait, 112th Congress.jpg
 | nominee2           = [[Mark Begich]]
 | party2             = Democratic Party (US)
 | popular_vote2      = 129,431
 | percentage2        = 45.8%

 | map_image          = 2014 United States Senate election in Alaska results map by borough and census area.svg
 | map_size           = 300px
| map_caption = '''Sullivan:''' {{legend0|#FFB2B2|40–50%}} {{legend0|#E27F7F|50–60%}} {{legend0|#d75d5d|60–70%}}<br/>'''Begich:''' {{legend0|#A5B0FF|40–50%}} {{legend0|#7996e2|50–60%}} {{legend0|#6674de|60–70%}} {{legend0|#584cde|70–80%}}

 | title              = U.S. Senator
 | before_election    = [[Mark Begich]]
 | before_party       = Democratic Party (US)
 | after_election     = [[Dan Sullivan (politician, born 1964)|Dan Sullivan]]
 | after_party        = Republican Party (US)
}}
{{Main|2014 United States Senate election in Alaska}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Alaska}}

One-term incumbent Democrat [[Mark Begich]] had been first elected with 48% of the vote in 2008, defeating six-term senator [[Ted Stevens]] by 3,953 votes (a margin of 1.25%).<ref name="BostonGlobe">{{Cite news |date=November 4, 2008 |title=Campaign 08 US Senate results |work=[[The Boston Globe]] |publisher=boston.com |url=http://www.boston.com/news/politics/2008/election_results/us_senate |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20121109061411/http://www.boston.com/news/politics/2008/election_results/us_senate |archive-date=November 9, 2012}}</ref> Begich was 52 years old in 2014 and was seeking re-election to a second term.<ref name="stevens">{{Cite news |date=April 8, 2009 |title=Stevens files candidacy for 2014 election |work=Anchorage Daily News |url=http://www.adn.com/ted-stevens/story/753646.html |url-status=dead |access-date=April 11, 2009 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20090417070216/http://www.adn.com/ted-stevens/story/753646.html |archive-date=April 17, 2009 |df=mdy-all}}</ref> Stevens, who would have been almost 91 years old at the time of the election, had already filed for a rematch back in 2009,<ref name=stevens/> but was [[2010 Alaska Turbo Otter crash|killed in a plane crash]] the following year.

Republican lieutenant governor [[Mead Treadwell]],<ref>{{Cite news |last=Adams |first=Eric Christopher |date=December 1, 2012 |title=Alaska lieutenant governor exploring US Senate run in 2014 |work=Alaska Dispatch |url=http://www.alaskadispatch.com/article/alaska-lieutenant-governor-exploring-us-senate-run-2014 |access-date=May 24, 2013 |archive-date=January 30, 2013 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20130130164605/http://www.alaskadispatch.com/article/alaska-lieutenant-governor-exploring-us-senate-run-2014 |url-status=dead }}</ref> [[2010 United States Senate election in Alaska|2010]] nominee [[Joe Miller (Alaska politician)|Joe Miller]],<ref>{{Cite news |last=Burns |first=Alexander |date=May 28, 2013 |title=Joe Miller files papers for Senate |work=Politico |url=https://www.politico.com/story/2013/05/joe-miller-alaska-senate-2014-91960.html}}</ref> State Natural Resources Commissioner [[Daniel S. Sullivan]],<ref name="Sullivan">{{Cite news |last=Trygstad |first=Kyle |date=September 12, 2013 |title=Alaska GOP Primary Set to Expand #AKSEN |work=Roll Call |url=http://atr.rollcall.com/alaska-gop-primary-set-to-expand |access-date=September 14, 2013 |archive-date=September 16, 2013 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20130916061740/http://atr.rollcall.com/alaska-gop-primary-set-to-expand/ |url-status=dead }}</ref> and Air Force veteran John Jaramillo ran for the GOP nomination. In the August 19 primary, Sullivan won the Republican nomination with 40% and defeated Begich in the general election.<ref>{{Cite web |title=Sullivan beats tea party in Alaska GOP Senate race |url=http://www.wggb.com/2014/08/20/sullivan-beats-tea-party-in-alaska-gop-senate-race |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20141018081846/http://www.wggb.com/2014/08/20/sullivan-beats-tea-party-in-alaska-gop-senate-race/ |archive-date=October 18, 2014 |access-date=October 27, 2014 |website=WGGB Springfield |df=mdy-all}}</ref>
{{clear}}

{{Main|2014 United States Senate election in Arkansas}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Arkansas}}

Two-term incumbent Democrat [[Mark Pryor]] had been re-elected with 80% of the vote without Republican opposition in 2008.<ref>{{Cite news |date=May 31, 2013 |title=Dem. Pryor: No one tells me what to do |publisher=cnn.com |url=http://politicalticker.blogs.cnn.com/2013/05/31/dem-pryor-no-one-tells-me-what-to-do|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20130706081309/http://politicalticker.blogs.cnn.com/2013/05/31/dem-pryor-no-one-tells-me-what-to-do/|url-status=dead|archive-date=July 6, 2013}}</ref> Pryor was running for a third term.<ref name="pryor">{{Cite news |date=April 19, 2012 |title=Sen. Mark Pryor is running for re-election in 2014 |publisher=Arkansas Times |url=http://www.arktimes.com/ArkansasBlog/archives/2012/04/19/sen-mark-pryor-is-running-for-re-election-in-2014}}</ref>

Freshman Representative [[Tom Cotton]] of [[Arkansas's 4th congressional district]] was the Republican nominee.<ref>{{Cite news |date=June 5, 2014 |title=2014 Arkansas Senate Primaries Results |publisher=politico.com |url=https://www.politico.com/2014-election/results/map/senate/arkansas/#.U5HrtfldV1Y}}</ref> In the general election, Cotton defeated Pryor.

One-term incumbent Democrat [[Mark Udall]] had been elected with 53% of the vote in 2008. Udall was running for re-election.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Wilson |first=Megan R. |date=January 28, 2013 |title=Dem senators form joint fundraising committee |url=https://thehill.com/blogs/ballot-box/fundraising/140484-dem-senators-form-joint-fundraising-committee/ |publisher=thehill.com}}</ref>

Congressman [[Cory Gardner]] of [[Colorado's 4th congressional district]] was the Republican nominee; his late entry into the race caused numerous Republicans to withdraw their candidacies.<ref name="Gardner">{{Cite news |last=Johnson |first=Hugh |date=April 1, 2014 |title=Mark Aspiri becomes the 5th Republican to drop out of the U.S. Senate race since Rep. Cory Gardner jumped in |work=[[The Denver Post]] |url=http://blogs.denverpost.com/thespot/2014/04/01/mark-aspiri-suspends-u-s-senate-bid/107680 |access-date=April 3, 2014}}</ref> Gaylon Kent was the Libertarian Party nominee. Unity Party of America founder and National Chairman Bill Hammons was the Unity Party nominee.

Democrat [[Chris Coons]] won in the [[2010 United States Senate special election in Delaware]] caused by [[Joe Biden]]'s election as [[Vice President of the United States|Vice President]], winning by a 57% to 41% margin. Coons sought re-election. His Republican opponent was engineer Kevin Wade,<ref>{{Cite web |title=Wade, Simpler win Republican primaries in Delaware, Mayrack gets Democratic nod for auditor |url=http://www.therepublic.com/view/story/2389184ee33041d5b9dfb028579b934b/DE--Delaware-Primary |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20140917022044/http://www.therepublic.com/view/story/2389184ee33041d5b9dfb028579b934b/DE--Delaware-Primary |archive-date=September 17, 2014}}</ref> whom Coons went on to defeat in the general election.

{{Main|2014 United States Senate election in Georgia}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Georgia}}

Two-term incumbent Republican [[Saxby Chambliss]] had been re-elected with 57% of the vote in 2008 in a [[runoff election]] with former state Representative [[Jim Martin (Georgia politician)|Jim Martin]]; Georgia requires run-off elections when no Senate candidate wins over 50% of the vote. Chambliss did not seek a third term.<ref name="chambliss">{{Cite news |date=January 25, 2013 |title=Report: Sen. Saxby Chambliss to retire |work=The Hill |url=https://thehill.com/blogs/ballot-box/senate-races/140321-gop-sen-saxby-chambliss-to-retire/}}</ref>

Political activist Derrick Grayson,<ref>{{Cite web |last=Blau |first=Max |date=May 23, 2013 |title=Meet Derrick Grayson, 'The Minister of Truth', yet another U.S. Senate candidate |url=http://clatl.com/freshloaf/archives/2013/05/23/meet-derrick-grayson-the-minister-of-truth-yet-another-us-senate-candidate |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20131007032217/http://clatl.com/freshloaf/archives/2013/05/23/meet-derrick-grayson-the-minister-of-truth-yet-another-us-senate-candidate |url-status=dead |archive-date=October 7, 2013 |access-date=May 23, 2013 |website=[[Creative Loafing (Atlanta)]]}}</ref> Representatives [[Jack Kingston]] of [[Georgia's 1st congressional district]],<ref>{{Cite news |last=Camia |first=Catalina |date=May 2, 2013 |title=Kingston joins crowded GOP field for Ga. Senate |publisher=[[USA Today]] |agency=Associated Press |url=https://www.usatoday.com/story/onpolitics/2013/05/02/kingston-georgia-senate-broun/2130485}}</ref> [[Paul Broun]] of [[Georgia's 10th congressional district]],<ref>{{Cite web |last1=Barrow |first1=Bill |author2-link=Associated Press |last2=Associated Press |date=February 6, 2013 |title=Paul Broun enters 2014 Senate race to succeed Saxby Chambliss |url=http://chronicle.augusta.com/news/government/2013-02-06/paul-broun-enters-2014-senate-race-succeed-saxby-chambliss |publisher=[[The Augusta Chronicle]]}}</ref> and [[Phil Gingrey]] of [[Georgia's 11th congressional district]]<ref>{{Cite web |last=Galloway |first=Jim |date=March 27, 2013 |title=Phil Gingrey joins 2014 contest for U.S. Senate |url=http://www.ajc.com/weblogs/political-insider/2013/mar/27/phil-gingrey-joins-2014-contest-us-senate |publisher=[[Atlanta Journal-Constitution]]}}</ref> all declared their candidacy for the Republican nomination, as did former secretary of state [[Karen Handel]].<ref>{{Cite news |last=Camila |first=Catalina |date=May 17, 2013 |title=Ex-Komen exec Karen Handel declares Ga. Senate bid |publisher=USA Today |url=https://www.usatoday.com/story/onpolitics/2013/05/17/karen-handel-senate-georgia-republican/2193343}}</ref> In the May 20 primary, no candidate received a majority of votes, so the top two candidates faced each other in a runoff; Perdue narrowly won against Kingston in the runoff primary election on July 22 with 50.9% of the vote.<ref>{{Cite web |date=July 22, 2014 |title=Perdue wins Georgia Senate runoff |url=https://www.politico.com/story/2014/07/georgia-election-2014-michelle-nunn-david-perdue-jack-kingston-109252.html |access-date=July 23, 2014 |publisher=[[Politico]]}}</ref>

[[Michelle Nunn]], CEO of [[Points of Light]] and the daughter of former U.S. Senator [[Sam Nunn]], won the Democratic nomination.<ref>{{Cite news |date=May 20, 2014 |title=In Georgia, Sen. Michelle Nunn wins Democratic primary; GOP race close |agency=Associated Press |url=http://www.timesfreepress.com/news/2014/may/20/georgia-sen-michelle-nunn-wins-democratic-primary-}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web |last1=Mike Owen |last2=McClatchy News Service |date=August 8, 2013 |title=Michelle Nunn, daughter of Sam Nunn, seeks U.S. Senate seat |url=http://www.mcclatchydc.com/2013/08/08/198877/michelle-nunn-daughter-of-sam.html#.UgwXF5KTgV0 |publisher=mcclatchydc.com|author2-link=McClatchy News Service }}</ref> Other declared Democratic candidates included former state senator [[Steen Miles]], psychiatrist Branko Radulovacki, and former US Army Ranger Todd Robinson. Amanda Swafford, a former [[Flowery Branch, Georgia]], city councilwoman, received the [[Libertarian Party of Georgia]] nomination.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Newton-Small |first=Jay |title=Meet the Woman Who Could Keep Control of the Senate Up for Grabs |url=https://time.com/3450155/georgia-senate-race-amanda-swafford/ |access-date=February 9, 2020 |website=[[Time (magazine)|Time]] |date=September 30, 2014 |publisher=TIME USA, LLC.}}</ref>

 | title              = U.S. Senator
 | before_election    = [[Brian Schatz]]
 | before_party       = Democratic Party (US)
 | after_election     = [[Brian Schatz]]
 | after_party        = Democratic Party (US)
}}
{{Main|2014 United States Senate special election in Hawaii}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Hawaii}}

[[Daniel Inouye]], the [[List of members of the United States Congress by longevity of service|second longest serving]] United States Senator in U.S. history, died on December 17, 2012, after respiratory complications.<ref>{{Cite news |date=December 17, 2012 |title=Sen. Daniel Inouye of Hawaii Dead at 88 |url=https://abcnews.go.com/Politics/wireStory/sen-daniel-inouye-hawaii-dead-88-18001608#.UM-neKU1ZSU}}</ref> [[Hawaii]] law allows the [[Governor of Hawaii]], to appoint an interim Senator "who serves until the next regularly-scheduled general election, chosen from a list of three prospective appointees that the prior incumbent's political party submits". Governor [[Neil Abercrombie]] did so,<ref>{{Cite news |date=January 21, 2009 |title=LAWS GOVERNING U.S. SENATE VACANCIES |url=https://www.cga.ct.gov/2009/rpt/2009-R-0018.htm |access-date=December 17, 2012}}</ref> selecting Lieutenant Governor [[Brian Schatz]] to fill the Senate seat.<ref>{{Cite news |last=Peters |first=Jeremy S. |date=December 26, 2012 |title=Hawaii's Lieutenant Governor to Succeed Inouye |work=[[The New York Times]] |url=https://www.nytimes.com/2012/12/27/us/politics/lt-gov-brian-schatz-of-hawaii-to-succeed-inouye-in-senate.html?_r=0}}</ref> Inouye had been re-elected in 2010 with 72% of the vote.<ref>{{Cite news |date=November 16, 2012 |title=GENERAL ELECTION – State of Hawaii |url=http://hawaii.gov/elections/results/2010/general/files/histatewide.pdf |access-date=December 17, 2012}}</ref> Schatz was challenged in the Democratic primary by Congresswoman [[Colleen Hanabusa]] of [[Hawaii's 1st congressional district]], who Inouye had hoped would be his successor.<ref>{{Cite news |last=Blake |first=Aaron |date=April 23, 2013 |title=Hanabusa will challenge Schatz in Hawaii Senate primary |newspaper=The Washington Post |url=https://www.washingtonpost.com/blogs/post-politics/wp/2013/04/23/hanabusa-will-challenge-schatz-in-hawaii-senate-primary}}</ref> Schatz defeated Hanabusa in the primary with 48.5% to 47.8%.<ref>{{Cite web |title=Hawaii Primary: Schatz Hangs On To Win US Senate Race |url=http://www.civilbeat.com/2014/08/hawaii-primary-schatz-hangs-on-to-win-us-senate-race |access-date=October 27, 2014 |website=Civil Beat|date=August 16, 2014 }}</ref>

[[Campbell Cavasso]], former state representative and nominee for the U.S. Senate in [[2004 United States Senate election in Hawaii|2004]] and [[2010 United States Senate election in Hawaii|2010]], was the Republican nominee.<ref>{{Cite news |last=Blair |first=Chad |date=October 22, 2013 |title=Civil Beat Poll — Schatz Has Narrow Lead Over Hanabusa |publisher=Honolulu Civil Beat |url=http://www.civilbeat.com/articles/2013/10/22/20203-civil-beat-poll-schatz-has-narrow-lead-over-hanabusa |access-date=October 23, 2013}}</ref>

Three-term incumbent and [[Assistant party leaders of the United States Senate|Senate Majority Whip]] Democrat [[Dick Durbin]] had been re-elected with 68% of the vote in 2008. Durbin ran for a fourth term.<ref>{{Cite news |last1=Livingston |first1=Abby |last2=Shiner |first2=Meredith |date=March 8, 2013 |title=Illinois: Durbin Will Run for Re-Election |work=Roll Call |publisher=rollcall.com |url=http://atr.rollcall.com/illinois-durbin-will-run-for-re-election |access-date=June 26, 2013 |archive-date=March 11, 2013 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20130311142833/http://atr.rollcall.com/illinois-durbin-will-run-for-re-election/ |url-status=dead }}</ref>

State Senator [[Jim Oberweis]] was the Republican nominee.<ref name="Oberweis">{{Cite news |last=Skiba |first=Katherine |date=March 19, 2014 |title=Oberweis faces tough race against Durbin |work=Chicago Tribune |url=https://www.chicagotribune.com/2014/03/19/oberweis-faces-tough-race-against-durbin/ |access-date=April 3, 2014}}</ref> He defeated primary challenger Doug Truax with 56% of the vote.

Five-term incumbent Democrat [[Tom Harkin]] had been re-elected with 63% of the vote in 2008. Harkin announced on January 26, 2013, that he would not seek a sixth term.<ref>{{Cite news |last=Kay Henderson |date=January 26, 2013 |title=Leading Senate liberal Harkin of Iowa to retire |work=Reuters |url=https://www.reuters.com/article/us-usa-politics-harkin/leading-senate-liberal-harkin-of-iowa-to-retire-idUSBRE90P0F120130126}}</ref> Congressman [[Bruce Braley]] was the Democratic nominee.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Brown |first=Alex |date=April 15, 2013 |title=Braley Has $1 Million On Hand, As Republicans Seek A Senate Candidate |url=http://www.nationaljournal.com/blogs/hotlineoncall/2013/04/braley-has-1-million-on-hand-as-republicans-seek-a-senate-candidate-15 |access-date=June 26, 2013 |work=nationaljournal.com}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web |title=Primary Election |url=http://sos.iowa.gov/elections/electioninfo/primary/index.html |access-date=March 21, 2014}}</ref>

State Senator [[Joni Ernst]] was the Republican nominee.<ref>{{Cite web |title=Iowa Senate: Jodi Ernst turns aim to Bruce Braley |website=[[The Des Moines Register]] |url=http://www.desmoinesregister.com/story/opinion/columnists/kathie-obradovich/2014/06/03/iowa-senate-joni-ernst-turns-aim-to-bruce-braley/9941807 |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://archive.today/20141105194609/http://www.desmoinesregister.com/story/opinion/columnists/kathie-obradovich/2014/06/03/iowa-senate-joni-ernst-turns-aim-to-bruce-braley/9941807 |archive-date=November 5, 2014 |access-date=June 4, 2014 |df=mdy-all}}</ref>

[[2014 United States Senate election in Iowa#Libertarian candidate|Doug Butzier]], who was the Libertarian Party's nominee, died in a plane crash on October 13, 2014, but still appeared on the ballot.<ref>{{Cite news |last=Staff |date=October 14, 2014 |title=Local doctor running for U.S. Senate killed in plane crash |work=Dubuque Telegraph Herald |url=http://www.thonline.com/news/breaking/article_fea46b32-53ad-11e4-882d-001a4bcf6878.html |access-date=October 14, 2014}}</ref>

| map_size = 270px
| map_caption = County results<br />'''Roberts:''' {{legend0|#ffb2b2|40–50%}} {{legend0|#e27f7f|50–60%}} {{legend0|#d75d5d|60–70%}} {{legend0|#d72f30|70–80%}} {{legend0|#c21b18|80–90%}}<br />'''Orman:''' {{legend0|#999999|50–60%}} {{legend0|#808080|60–70%}}

{{Main|2014 United States Senate election in Kansas}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Kansas}}

Three-term incumbent Republican [[Pat Roberts]] had been re-elected with 60% of the vote in 2008. Roberts sought a fourth term.<ref name="roberts">{{Cite news |last=Clarkin |first=Mary |date=October 31, 2009 |title=Roberts already planning 2014 bid |work=The Hutchinson News |publisher=hutchnews.com |url=http://www.hutchnews.com/Localregional/roberts2009-10-31T20-49-31 |url-status=dead |access-date=June 26, 2013 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20131103101519/http://www.hutchnews.com/Localregional/roberts2009-10-31T20-49-31 |archive-date=November 3, 2013 |df=mdy-all}}</ref> He faced a primary challenge from radiologist Milton Wolf, a conservative [[Tea Party movement|Tea Party]] supporter.<ref>{{Cite news |last=Alman |first=Ashley |date=October 9, 2013 |title=Tea Party Activist To Challenge Roberts In 2014 |work=Huffington Post |url=http://www.huffingtonpost.com/2013/10/09/milton-wolf-us-senate_n_4067803.html}}</ref> Roberts defeated Wolf in the Republican primary by 48% to 41%.<ref>{{Cite news |last=Cillizza |first=Chris |date=August 7, 2014 |title=Pat Roberts won the Kansas primary on Tuesday. One Fix reader called it. |newspaper=[[The Washington Post]] |url=https://www.washingtonpost.com/news/the-fix/wp/2014/08/07/pat-roberts-won-the-kansas-primary-on-tuesday-one-fix-reader-called-it/ |access-date=October 27, 2014}}</ref> [[Shawnee County, Kansas|Shawnee County]] District Attorney [[Chad Taylor (Kansas)|Chad Taylor]] won the Democratic nomination.<ref name="Taylor">{{Cite news |last=Haake |first=Garrett |date=February 27, 2014 |title=Kansas Senate race heats up with new entry and endorsements |work=KSHB 41 |url=http://www.kshb.com/news/political/kansas-senate-race-heats-up-with-new-entry-and-endorsements |url-status=dead |access-date=February 28, 2014 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20140304151751/http://www.kshb.com/news/political/kansas-senate-race-heats-up-with-new-entry-and-endorsements |archive-date=March 4, 2014}}</ref> Randall Batson from Wichita was on the general election ballot as a Libertarian.<ref>{{Cite web |last=McMillin |first=Molly |title=Kansas Libertarians choose election slate |url=http://www.kansas.com/2014/04/26/3425042/kansas-libertarians-choose-election.html |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20140812211509/http://www.kansas.com/2014/04/26/3425042/kansas-libertarians-choose-election.html |archive-date=August 12, 2014 |access-date=June 27, 2014 |publisher=Kansas.com}}</ref> Also, [[Greg Orman]] qualified for the ballot as an [[Independent (politician)|independent]].<ref name="hancock">{{Cite news |last=Hancock |first=Peter |date=July 28, 2014 |title=Senate challenger says he has enough signatures to get on ballot as independent candidate |work=Lawrence Journal-World |url=http://www2.ljworld.com/news/2014/jul/28/senate-challenger-says-he-has-enough-signatures-ge}}</ref>

On September 3, Taylor announced he was dropping out of the election, leading to speculation that Democrats would support Orman's candidacy.<ref name="Washington Post">{{Cite news |last=Sullivan |first=Sean |date=September 3, 2014 |title=Democratic nominee Chad Taylor drops out of Kansas Senate race |newspaper=[[The Washington Post]] |url=https://www.washingtonpost.com/blogs/post-politics/wp/2014/09/03/democratic-nominee-chad-taylor-drops-out-of-kansas-senate-race |access-date=October 27, 2014}}</ref> On September 18, the [[Kansas Supreme Court]] ruled that Taylor's name had to be removed from the ballot.<ref>{{Cite news |last=Mitch Smith |date=September 18, 2014 |title=Kansas Justices Back Senate Candidate's Withdrawal |work=The New York Times |url=https://www.nytimes.com/2014/09/19/us/kansas-democrats-withdrawal-from-senate-race-is-upheld.html?ref=politics}}</ref>

{{Main|2014 United States Senate election in Kentucky}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Kentucky}}

Five-term Republican incumbent and [[Party leaders of the United States Senate|Senate Minority Leader]] [[Mitch McConnell]] had been re-elected with 53% of the vote in 2008. McConnell sought re-election to a sixth term.<ref name="mcconnell">{{Cite news |date=December 20, 2010 |title=Mitch McConnell plans power plays against Democrats |work=[[Politico]] |publisher=Politico.com |url=https://www.politico.com/news/stories/1210/46642_Page2.html}}</ref> McConnell defeated businessman [[Matt Bevin]] in the Republican primary on May 20.<ref name="kyprim">{{Cite web |date=May 21, 2014 |title=McConnell crushes Bevin; Grimes cruises to win |url=http://www.courier-journal.com/story/news/politics/elections/kentucky/2014/05/20/ap-mcconnell-grimes-claim-senate-primaries/9350463 |publisher=Courier-Journal}}</ref>

[[Kentucky]] [[Secretary of State of Kentucky|Secretary of State]] [[Alison Lundergan Grimes]], with support from much of Kentucky's Democratic leadership, won the Democratic primary.<ref name=kyprim/><ref>{{Cite web |last=Joseph Gerth |date=July 31, 2013 |title=Alison Lundergan Grimes blasts Mitch McConnell at campaign rollout |url=http://www.courier-journal.com/article/20130730/NEWS010605/307300087/Alison-Lundergan-Grimes-blasts-Mitch-McConnell-campaign-rollout |publisher=courier-journal.com}}</ref> Actress [[Ashley Judd]] publicly claimed to be considering a run for the Democratic nomination, but ultimately decided against it.<ref>{{Cite news |last=Trip Gabriel |date=March 27, 2013 |title=Ashley Judd Passes on Senate Run in Kentucky |work=[[The New York Times]] |url=http://thecaucus.blogs.nytimes.com/2013/03/27/ashley-judd-passes-on-senate-run-in-kentucky/?_r=0}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web |last=Manu Raju |date=December 4, 2012 |title=Ashley Judd exploring Senate run |url=https://www.politico.com/story/2012/12/ashley-judd-exploring-senate-run-84542.html |publisher=politico.com}}</ref>

Ed Marksberry pursued an independent bid after dropping out of the Democratic field in September 2013.<ref name="wfpl.org">{{Cite web |last=Bailey |first=Phillip |date=July 5, 2013 |title=Senate Candidates Decry Kentucky Democratic Party E-mail Promoting Alison Lundergan Grimes |url=http://wfpl.org/post/senate-candidates-decry-kentucky-democratic-party-e-mail-promoting-alison-lundergan-grimes |access-date=November 29, 2013}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web |last=Alessi |first=Ryan |date=September 23, 2013 |title=Ed Marksberry to drop out of Democratic U.S. Senate primary to run as independent |url=http://mycn2.com/politics/ed-marksberry-to-drop-out-of-democratic-us-senate-primary-to-run-as-independent |access-date=February 22, 2014 |website=mycn2.com}}</ref>

== Louisiana ==
{{Infobox election

| map = {{switcher |[[File:2014 United States Senate election in Louisiana results map by parish.svg|280px]] |First round parish results |[[File:Louisiana Senate Runoff Election Results by County, 2014.svg|280px]] |Runoff parish results |default=2}}
| map_caption = '''Cassidy:''' {{legend0|#FFC8CD|30–40%}} {{legend0|#ffb2b2|40–50%}} {{legend0|#e27f7f|50–60%}} {{legend0|#d75d5d|60–70%}} {{legend0|#d72f30|70–80%}} {{legend0|#c21b18|80–90%}}<br />'''Landrieu:''' {{legend0|#a5b0ff|40–50%}} {{legend0|#7996e2|50–60%}} {{legend0|#6674de|60–70%}} {{legend0|#3933e5|80–90%}}

{{Main|2014 United States Senate election in Louisiana}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Louisiana}}

Three-term incumbent Democrat [[Mary Landrieu]] had been re-elected with 52% of the vote in 2008. Landrieu ran for a fourth term.<ref name="landrieu">{{Cite news |date=February 5, 2011 |title=Sen. Mary Landrieu is seeking a fourth Senate term |work=The Times-Picayune |url=http://www.nola.com/politics/index.ssf/2011/02/sen_mary_landrieu_plans_to_see.html|archive-url=https://archive.today/20130130021204/http://www.nola.com/politics/index.ssf/2011/02/sen_mary_landrieu_plans_to_see.html|url-status=dead|archive-date=January 30, 2013}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web |last=Costanza |first=Rusty |title=Sen. Mary Landrieu is in a tenuous position as the last statewide-elected Democrat in Louisiana |url=http://www.nola.com/politics/index.ssf/2011/07/sen_mary_landrieu_is_in_tenuou.html |archive-url=https://archive.today/20120912060809/http://www.nola.com/politics/index.ssf/2011/07/sen_mary_landrieu_is_in_tenuou.html |url-status=dead |archive-date=September 12, 2012 |access-date=November 24, 2012 |publisher=NOLA.com}}</ref>

Louisiana uses a unique [[jungle primary]] system that eschews primaries in favor of [[run-off election]]s between the top two candidates; this run-off can be avoided if the winning candidate receives over 50% of the vote. Democrats Wayne Ables, Vallian Senegal, and William Waymire ran against Landrieu in the election, as did Republicans [[Bill Cassidy]] (representative of [[Louisiana's 6th congressional district]]), Thomas Clements (small business owner), and retired [[United States Air Force|Air Force]] [[Colonel]] Rob Maness.<ref>{{Cite news |last=Rachel Weiner |date=April 2, 2013 |title=Rep. Bill Cassidy announces Senate bid |work=washingtonpost.com |url=https://www.washingtonpost.com/blogs/post-politics/wp/2013/04/02/rep-bill-cassidy-announces-senate-bid|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20130407095816/http://www.washingtonpost.com/blogs/post-politics/wp/2013/04/02/rep-bill-cassidy-announces-senate-bid/|url-status=dead|archive-date=April 7, 2013}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web |last=Drew Broach |date=May 13, 2013 |title=Sen. Mary Landrieu's new challenger: Air Force veteran from Madisonville |url=http://www.nola.com/politics/index.ssf/2013/05/us_senate_race_attracts_air_fo.html |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20130608070805/http://www.nola.com/politics/index.ssf/2013/05/us_senate_race_attracts_air_fo.html |url-status=dead |archive-date=June 8, 2013 |publisher=nola.com}}</ref> [[Electrical Engineer]] Brannon McMorris ran as a Libertarian.<ref name="US Elections~Louisiana">{{Cite web |title=2020 Louisiana Elections, Candidates, Races and Voting |url=https://uselections.com/la/la.htm |website=uselections.com}}</ref>

Because Republican candidate Maness took almost 14% of the votes in the primary, there was a runoff election on December 6, 2014, between Landrieu (42%) and Cassidy (41%). Cassidy won the runoff with 56% of the vote.

{{Main|2014 United States Senate election in Maine}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Maine}}

Three-term incumbent Republican [[Susan Collins]] was seeking a fourth term.<ref>{{Cite news |last=Camia |first=Catalina |date=March 20, 2013 |title=Moderate GOP Sen. Collins intends to run again; USA Today |publisher=usatoday.com |url=https://www.usatoday.com/story/news/politics/2013/03/20/susan-collins-senate-2014-election-maine/2003837 |access-date=April 3, 2013}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web |date=August 29, 2010 |title=Sen. Susan Collins has become a key player in Congress |url=http://www.kjonline.com/news/growing-influencesen_-susan-collins-has-becomea-key-player-in-congress_2010-08-28.html |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20120312153811/http://www.kjonline.com/news/growing-influencesen_-susan-collins-has-becomea-key-player-in-congress_2010-08-28.html |archive-date=March 12, 2012 |access-date=November 24, 2012 |publisher=Kjonline.com |df=mdy-all}}</ref> [[Shenna Bellows]], former executive director of the [[American Civil Liberties Union]] of Maine, was the Democratic nominee.<ref>{{Cite web |date=October 8, 2013 |title=Shenna Bellows to launch U.S. Senate campaign on Oct. 23 |url=http://www.kjonline.com/politics/Shenna_Bellows_to_launch_U_S__Senate_campaing_on_Oct__23_.html |access-date=October 8, 2013 |publisher=[[Kennebec Journal]]}}</ref>

== Massachusetts ==
{{Infobox election

{{Main|2014 United States Senate election in Massachusetts}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Massachusetts}}
Five-term incumbent and 2004 Democratic presidential nominee [[John Kerry]] had been re-elected with 66% of the vote in 2008. Kerry resigned in early 2013 to become [[U.S. Secretary of State]].<ref>{{Cite news |last=Rogers |first=Alex |date=January 30, 2013 |title=John Kerry Bids Farewell to the Senate After 28 Years |publisher=[[Time (magazine)|Time]] |url=https://swampland.time.com/2013/01/30/john-kerry-bids-farewell-to-the-senate-after-28-years/}}</ref> Governor [[Deval Patrick]] appointed Democrat [[Mo Cowan]] to the seat.<ref>{{Cite news |last=Linskey |first=Annie |date=January 30, 2013 |title=Patrick Appoints Former Staff Chief Cowan to Kerry Senate |publisher=bloomberg.com |url=https://www.bloomberg.com/news/2013-01-30/patrick-appoints-longtime-donor-fulp-to-kerry-senate-seat.html}}</ref> Democratic Congressman [[Ed Markey]] beat Republican [[Gabriel E. Gomez]] in the [[2013 United States Senate special election in Massachusetts|June 25, 2013 special election]] by a 55% to 45% margin.<ref>{{Cite news |last=Seelye |first=Katharine Q. |date=April 30, 2013 |title=Senior Congressman and Newcomer Win Senate Nods in Massachusetts |work=[[The New York Times]] |url=https://www.nytimes.com/2013/05/01/us/massachusetts-kerry-senate-replacement.html}}</ref> Markey had served the remainder of Kerry's term before running for re-election to a first full term in 2014. [[Hopkinton, Massachusetts|Hopkinton]] Town Selectman Brian Herr was the Republican nominee.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Schoenberg |first=Shira |title=Republican Hopkinton selectman Brian Herr to challenge U.S. Sen. Ed Markey |url=https://www.masslive.com/politics/2014/01/republican_hopkinton_selectman.html |access-date=February 9, 2020 |website=masslive.com |date=January 30, 2014 |publisher=Advance Local Media, LLC.}}</ref>

{{Main|2014 United States Senate election in Michigan}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Michigan}}

Six-term incumbent senator and Chairman of the Armed Services Committee Democrat [[Carl Levin]], the longest-serving senator in Michigan's history, had been re-elected with 63% of the vote in 2008. Levin announced on March 7, 2013, that he would not seek re-election.

Three-term Democratic representative [[Gary Peters]] of [[Michigan's 14th congressional district|MI-14]] was the Democratic nominee.<ref>{{Cite news |last=Klug |first=Fritz |date=May 23, 2013 |title=U.S. Senators Carl Levin, Debbie Stabenow endorse Gary Peters in 2014 U.S. Senate election |work=M Live |url=http://www.mlive.com/news/index.ssf/2013/05/us_senators_carl_levin_debbie.html}}</ref> He defeated Republican former secretary of state [[Terri Lynn Land]] who was unopposed for the Republican nomination.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Egan |first=Paul |date=June 4, 2013 |title=Republican Terri Lynn Land joins U.S. Senate race |url=http://www.freep.com/article/20130603/NEWS15/306030050/Republican-Terri-Lynn-Land-joins-U-S-Senate-race |publisher=[[Detroit Free Press]]}}</ref>

== Minnesota ==
{{Infobox election
 | election_name      = Minnesota election
 | country            = Minnesota
 | type               = presidential
 | ongoing            = no
 | previous_election  = 2008 United States Senate election in Minnesota
 | previous_year      = 2008
 | next_election      = 2018 United States Senate special election in Minnesota
 | next_year          = 2018 (special)
 | image_size         = 125x136px

 | map_image          = 2014 United States Senate election in Minnesota results map by county.svg
 | map_size           = 250px
| map_caption = '''Franken:''' {{legend0|#a5b0ff|40-50%}} {{legend0|#7996e2|50-60%}} {{legend0|#6674de|60-70%}}<br />'''McFadden:''' {{legend0|#ffb2b2|40-50%}} {{legend0|#e27f7f|50-60%}}

 | image1             = Al Franken, official portrait, 114th Congress (cropped).jpg
 | nominee1           = '''[[Al Franken]]'''
 | party1             = Democratic–Farmer–Labor Party (Minnesota)
 | popular_vote1      = '''1,053,205'''

 | image2             = Mike McFadden, CD4 Convention, April 2014.png
 | nominee2           = [[Mike McFadden]]
 | party2             = Republican Party (Minnesota)
 | popular_vote2      = 850,227
 | percentage2        = 42.9%

 | title              = U.S. Senator
 | before_election    = [[Al Franken]]
 | before_party       = Democratic–Farmer–Labor Party (Minnesota)
 | after_election     = [[Al Franken]]
 | after_party        = Democratic–Farmer–Labor Party (Minnesota)
}}
{{Main|2014 United States Senate election in Minnesota}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Minnesota}}

One-term incumbent Democrat [[Al Franken]] unseated one-term Republican [[Norm Coleman]] by 312 votes in a contested three-way race with 42% of the vote in 2008; the third candidate in the race, former senator [[Dean Barkley]] of the [[Independence Party of Minnesota]], won 15% of the vote.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Weiner |first=Jay |date=April 13, 2009 |title=Judges' 'three votes' give Al Franken convincing win in Senate recount trial |url=http://www.minnpost.com/politics-policy/2009/04/judges%E2%80%99-%E2%80%98three-votes%E2%80%99-give-al-franken-convincing-win-senate-recount-trial |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20131102160936/http://www.minnpost.com/politics-policy/2009/04/judges%E2%80%99-%E2%80%98three-votes%E2%80%99-give-al-franken-convincing-win-senate-recount-trial |archive-date=November 2, 2013 |access-date=February 7, 2014 |website=[[MinnPost]] |df=mdy-all}}</ref> Franken sought re-election.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Richert |first=Catharine |date=August 12, 2012 |title=Franken creates potent fundraising machine in advance of re-election bid |url=http://minnesota.publicradio.org/display/web/2012/08/15/politics/al-franken-fundraising |access-date=February 8, 2013 |website=[[Minnesota Public Radio]]}}</ref> State Representative [[Jim Abeler]],<ref name="abeler">{{Cite web |last=Pugmire, Tim and Tom Scheck |date=June 18, 2013 |title=State Rep. Abeler running for US Senate |url=http://minnesota.publicradio.org/display/web/2013/06/18/politics/abeler-senate |access-date=June 18, 2013 |website=[[Minnesota Public Radio]]}}</ref> [[St. Louis County, Minnesota|St. Louis County]] Commissioner Chris Dahlberg,<ref name="dahlberg">{{Cite web |date=August 20, 2013 |title=St. Louis County commissioner announces run for U.S. Senate |url=http://www.duluthnewstribune.com/event/article/id/275707 |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20130821001158/http://www.duluthnewstribune.com/event/article/id/275707/ |archive-date=August 21, 2013 |access-date=August 21, 2013 |website=[[Duluth News Tribune]]}}</ref> co-CEO of Lazard Middle Market [[Mike McFadden]], bison farmer and former hair salon owner Monti Moreno,<ref name="moreno">{{Cite web |last=Zdechlik, Mark |date=September 12, 2013 |title=Monti Moreno enters Senate race, without musket |url=http://blogs.mprnews.org/capitol-view/2013/09/monti-moreno-enters-senate-race-without-musket |access-date=February 3, 2014 |website=[[Minnesota Public Radio]]}}</ref> state Senator [[Julianne Ortman]],<ref name="ortman">{{Cite web |date=July 31, 2013 |title=GOP state senator Ortman to announce plans for US Senate race at weekend event |url=http://www.startribune.com/politics/national/217857531.html |access-date=August 1, 2013 |website=[[Star Tribune]]}}</ref> and [[United States Navy Reserve|U.S. Navy reservist]] Phillip Parrish<ref name="parrish">{{Cite web |last=Carlson, Heather J. |date=January 17, 2014 |title=GOP Senate candidates face-off in tea party forum |url=http://ww3.postbulletin.com/news/stories/display.php?id=1547029 |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20140222100427/http://ww3.postbulletin.com/news/stories/display.php?id=1547029 |archive-date=February 22, 2014 |access-date=February 7, 2014 |website=[[Post-Bulletin]] |df=mdy-all}}</ref> ran for the Republican nomination. McFadden won the Republican primary and was the Republican nominee in the general election.<ref>{{Cite web |title=Businessman Mike McFadden wins Minnesota's GOP Senate primary; takes on Al Franken in November |website=[[Star Tribune]] |url=http://www.startribune.com/local/271008111.html |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20140821090242/http://www.startribune.com/local/271008111.html |archive-date=August 21, 2014 |access-date=October 27, 2014}}</ref>

Hannah Nicollet of the [[Independence Party of Minnesota]] also ran.<ref name="nicollet">{{Cite web |last=Scheck, Tom |date=February 6, 2014 |title=Ron Paul backer announces Minn. Senate bid as Independence Party candidate |url=http://www.mprnews.org/story/2014/02/06/hannah-nicollet-senate-bid |access-date=February 7, 2014 |website=[[Minnesota Public Radio]]}}</ref>

 | before_party       = Republican Party (US)
 | after_election     = [[Thad Cochran]]
 | after_party        = Republican Party (US)
}}

{{Main|2014 United States Senate election in Mississippi}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Mississippi}}

Six-term incumbent Republican [[Thad Cochran]], re-elected with 62% of the vote in 2008, ran for re-election.<ref name="Running" /> Cochran was the last incumbent senator to declare his plans, leading to widespread speculation that he might announce his retirement.<ref name="Guessing">{{Cite news |date=August 8, 2013 |title=Mississippi Senate race 2014: Guessing game over Thad Cochran run |work=Politico |url=https://www.politico.com/story/2013/08/mississippi-senate-race-thad-cochran-95350.html |access-date=August 9, 2013}}</ref><ref name="Cochran unknown">{{Cite web |last=Raju |first=Manu |date=May 20, 2013 |title=Thad Cochran: Too early on 2014 |url=https://www.politico.com/blogs/on-congress/2013/03/thad-cochran-too-early-on-2014-159825 |publisher=politico.com}}</ref>
[[Tea Party Movement|Tea Party]] candidate [[Chris McDaniel]], a conservative Mississippi state senator, ran against Cochran in the Republican primary.<ref name="McDaniel">{{Cite news |last=Livingston |first=Abby |date=October 17, 2013 |title=Tea Party Candidate Challenges Thad Cochran |work=Roll Call |url=http://atr.rollcall.com/tea-party-candidate-challenges-thad-cochran |access-date=October 17, 2013 |archive-date=October 18, 2013 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20131018045810/http://atr.rollcall.com/tea-party-candidate-challenges-thad-cochran/ |url-status=dead }}</ref> Neither McDaniel nor Cochran was able to get 50% of the vote in the first round of the primary, so a [[runoff election]] was held June 24.<ref>{{Cite news |last=Martin |first=Jonathan |date=June 4, 2014 |title=Mississippi's G.O.P. Senate Primary Headed to a Runoff |work=The New York Times |url=https://www.nytimes.com/2014/06/05/us/politics/cochran-and-mcdaniel-mississippi-primary-runoff.html |access-date=June 4, 2014}}</ref> Cochran won the runoff election by 51% to 49%, with the help of Democratic voters eligible to vote in the state's open primaries who chose Cochran as their preferred Republican.<ref>{{Cite news |last=Hook |first=Janet |date=June 25, 2014 |title=Thad Cochran Defeats Tea Party-Backed Challenger in Mississippi GOP Senate Runoff |work=WSJ |url=https://online.wsj.com/articles/thad-cochran-defeats-tea-party-backed-challenger-in-mississippi-gop-senate-runoff-1403666748 |access-date=October 27, 2014}}</ref> McDaniel filed a lawsuit to challenge the results of the run-off, but the [[Chris McDaniel#2014 U.S. Senate campaign|challenge]] was rejected on appeal by the [[Supreme Court of Mississippi]].<ref name="McDaniel appeal fails">{{Cite news |last=Le Coz |first=Emily |date=October 24, 2014 |title=Mississippi Supreme Court rejects McDaniel Senate primary challenge |publisher=Reuters |url=https://www.reuters.com/article/us-usa-mississippi-election-idUSKCN0ID2LJ20141024 |access-date=November 5, 2014}}</ref>

Former Congressman [[Travis Childers]] was the Democratic nominee.<ref name="DJournal">{{Cite news |last=Harrison |first=Bobby |date=February 28, 2014 |title=Childers Running for Senate |work=Daily Journal |url=https://www.djournal.com/travis-childers-running-for-senate/article_6b94bea3-a883-531e-824b-8518759d2591.html |url-status=live |access-date=February 28, 2014 |url-access=subscription |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20140228233102/http://djournal.com/news/childers-running-senate/ |archive-date=February 28, 2014}}</ref>

== Montana ==

{{Main|2014 United States Senate election in Montana}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Montana}}

Six-term incumbent Democrat [[Max Baucus]], the longest-serving senator in Montana's history, had been re-elected with 73% of the vote in 2008. Baucus announced on April 23, 2013, that he would retire in 2014, rather than seek re-election to a seventh term.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Henderson |first=Greg |date=April 23, 2013 |title=Montana's Max Baucus To Retire; Republicans Eye 2014 Chances |url=https://www.npr.org/blogs/itsallpolitics/2013/04/23/178597359/montanas-max-baucus-to-retire-republicans-eye-2014-chances |publisher=npr.org}}</ref> Baucus was appointed as the [[United States Ambassador to China]], leading him to resign from the Senate in February 2014.<ref>{{Cite news |last=O'Keefe |first=Ed |date=February 6, 2014 |title=Max Baucus confirmed as U.S. ambassador to China |work=washingtonpost.com |url=https://www.washingtonpost.com/news/post-politics/wp/2014/02/06/max-baucus-confirmed-as-u-s-ambassador-to-china/}}</ref>

Following Baucus's confirmation as ambassador, Governor [[Steve Bullock (American politician)|Steve Bullock]] appointed the Lieutenant Governor [[John Walsh (Montana politician)|John Walsh]] to fill the vacant senate seat.<ref>{{Cite news |last=Camia |first=Catalina |date=February 7, 2014 |title=Montana gov taps John Walsh to replace Baucus in Senate |publisher=usatoday.com |url=https://www.usatoday.com/story/news/politics/2014/02/07/walsh-montana-senate-baucus-china/5278535}}</ref> Walsh went on to defeat former lieutenant governor [[John Bohlinger]] in the Democratic primary, but later pulled out of the race amid controversy over alleged plagiarism in a 2007 research paper.<ref>{{Cite news |title=Statement from U.S. Senator John Walsh |url=http://johnwalsh2014.com |url-status=usurped |access-date=November 7, 2014 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20140214040804/http://johnwalsh2014.com/ |archive-date=February 14, 2014}}</ref> The [[Montana Democratic Party]] held a special nominating convention on August 16 to choose a replacement for Walsh. First-term state representative [[Amanda Curtis]] won the nomination, thereby becoming the new Democratic nominee.<ref>{{Cite web |date=August 16, 2014 |title=Rep. Amanda Curtis: Women's rights, labor activist |url=http://www.sfgate.com/news/article/Rep-Amanda-Curtis-Women-s-rights-labor-activist-5693033.php |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20140816183336/http://www.sfgate.com/news/article/Rep-Amanda-Curtis-Women-s-rights-labor-activist-5693033.php |archive-date=August 16, 2014 |access-date=October 27, 2014 |website=SFGate}}</ref>

Congressman [[Steve Daines]] won the Republican nomination<ref>{{Cite web |date=November 6, 2013 |title=Montana GOP congressman announces US Senate bid |url=https://news.yahoo.com/montana-gop-congressman-announces-us-senate-bid-193717165--election.html |access-date=October 27, 2014 |website=Yahoo News}}</ref> over state Representative [[Champ Edmunds]] of [[Missoula, Montana|Missoula]] and David Leaser of [[Kalispell, Montana|Kalispell]].{{Election box begin no change
| title       = Montana Democratic primary election<ref name="MTprimaryresults">{{Cite web |title=2014 Statewide Montana Primary Election Canvas |url=http://sos.mt.gov/elections/2014/2014-Primary-Official-Statewide-Canvass.pdf |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20140728143746/http://sos.mt.gov/Elections/2014/2014-Primary-Official-Statewide-Canvass.pdf |archive-date=July 28, 2014 |access-date=July 23, 2014 |publisher=Montana Secretary of State |df=mdy-all}}</ref>
}}

{{Main|2014 United States Senate election in Nebraska}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Nebraska}}

One-term incumbent Republican [[Mike Johanns]] had been elected with 58% of the vote in 2008. He did not seek a second term.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Lipton |first=Eric |date=February 18, 2013 |title=Sen. Mike Johanns Of Nebraska To Retire In 2014 |url=http://www.ibtimes.com/sen-mike-johanns-nebraska-retire-2014-1092152 |work=[[International Business Times]]}}</ref> Term-limited Republican governor [[Dave Heineman]] considered running for the Republican nomination, but ultimately decided not to do so.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Becker |first=Bernie |date=May 25, 2013 |title=Neb. Gov. Heineman says no to Senate bid |url=https://thehill.com/blogs/ballot-box/senate-races/151672-neb-gov-heineman-says-no-to-senate-bid/ |access-date=May 29, 2013}}</ref> Former state Treasurer [[Shane Osborn]],<ref>{{Cite web |last=Walton |first=Don |date=June 2, 2013 |title=Shane Osborn enters GOP Senate race |url=http://journalstar.com/news/local/shane-osborn-enters-gop-senate-race/article_b758eb71-76d7-5e78-8e3a-6c36a1bccbf9.html |publisher=journalstar.com}}</ref> attorney Bart McLeay, banker Sid Dinsdale, and [[Midland University]] President [[Ben Sasse]] ran for the Republican nomination.<ref>{{Cite web |last1=Robynn Tysver |last2=Omaha World-Herald |title=Attorney Bart McLeay to run for Nebraska U.S. Senate seat |url=http://www.omaha.com/article/20130701/NEWS/707029977/1694 |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://archive.today/20130708154214/http://www.omaha.com/article/20130701/NEWS/707029977/1694 |archive-date=July 8, 2013 |access-date=November 7, 2014 |publisher=omaha.com |df=mdy-all|author2-link=Omaha World-Herald }}</ref><ref>{{Cite news |date=July 23, 2013 |title=Midland University president Sasse files for Senate |work=omaha.com |publisher=[[Omaha World-Herald]] |agency=Associated Press |url=http://www.omaha.com/article/20130723/NEWS/130729545/1694#midland-university-president-sasse-files-for-senate |url-status=dead |access-date=August 20, 2013 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20130928090138/http://www.omaha.com/article/20130723/NEWS/130729545/1694#midland-university-president-sasse-files-for-senate |archive-date=September 28, 2013 |df=mdy-all}}</ref> In the May 13 primary, Sasse won the Republican nomination.

[[Trial lawyer]] [[David Domina]] was the Democratic nominee.<ref>{{Cite news |date=May 13, 2014 |title=Dave Domina Wins Nebraska Senate Primary |work=The Huffington Post |url=http://www.huffingtonpost.com/2014/05/13/dave-domina-primary_n_5320260.html}}</ref>

{{Infobox election

 | image2             = Sbrownofficial.jpg

{{Main|2014 United States Senate election in New Hampshire}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from New Hampshire}}

One-term incumbent Democrat [[Jeanne Shaheen]] had been elected with 52% of the vote in 2008. Shaheen ran for re-election.<ref name="Shaheen">{{Cite web |date=March 8, 2012 |title=Dover NH, Rochester NH, Portsmouth NH, Laconia NH, Sanford ME |url=http://www.fosters.com/apps/pbcs.dll/article?AID=/20120308/GJNEWS_01/703089867 |access-date=November 24, 2012 |publisher=Fosters.com |archive-date=June 15, 2013 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20130615152646/http://www.fosters.com/apps/pbcs.dll/article?AID=/20120308/GJNEWS_01/703089867 |url-status=dead }}</ref> Shaheen defeated Republican nominee [[Scott Brown (politician)|Scott Brown]], who had represented neighboring Massachusetts in the [[United States Senate|Senate]] from 2010 to 2013.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Steven R. Hurst |date=September 9, 2014 |title=Scott Brown wins N.H. primary bid for US Senate (+video) |url=https://www.csmonitor.com/USA/Latest-News-Wires/2014/0909/Scott-Brown-wins-N.H.-primary-bid-for-US-Senate-video |access-date=October 27, 2014 |website=The Christian Science Monitor}}</ref>

{{Main|2014 United States Senate election in New Jersey}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from New Jersey|2013 United States Senate special election in New Jersey}}
Incumbent Democrat [[Frank Lautenberg]] had been re-elected with 56% of the vote in 2008. After announcing he would not seek re-election, Lautenberg died in June 2013, aged 89, after a long period of ill health.<ref name="lautenbergobit">{{Cite news |last=Clymer |first=Adam |date=June 3, 2013 |title=Frank Lautenberg, New Jersey Senator in His 5th Term, Dies at 89 |work=[[The New York Times]] |url=https://www.nytimes.com/2013/06/04/nyregion/frank-lautenberg-new-jersey-senator.html?pagewanted=all |access-date=November 7, 2014}}</ref><ref name="guardiannewjersey">{{Cite news |last=Williams |first=Matt |date=June 3, 2013 |title=New Jersey senator Frank Lautenberg dies aged 89 |work=The Guardian |location=London, UK |url=https://www.theguardian.com/world/2013/jun/03/frank-lautenberg-new-jersey-senator-dies |access-date=November 7, 2014}}</ref>

[[Mayors of Newark, New Jersey|Newark Mayor]] [[Cory Booker]], a Democrat, defeated Republican nominee [[Steve Lonegan]] by 55%-to-45% in a [[2013 United States Senate special election in New Jersey|2013 special election]] to replace interim Republican appointee [[Jeffrey Chiesa]].<ref>{{Cite news |date=2013 |title=New Jersey Senate-2013 Election Results |work=The New York Times |url=https://www.nytimes.com/projects/elections/2013/nj-special/senate/map.html?smid=tw-nytimes |access-date=November 7, 2014}}</ref> Booker ran for re-election to a full term in 2014. [[1978 United States Senate elections|1978]] and [[1982 United States Senate elections|1982]] Republican candidate and political operative [[Jeff Bell (politician)|Jeff Bell]] was the Republican nominee.<ref>{{Cite web |title=Jeff Bell for Senate |url=http://www.nysun.com/editorials/jeff-bell-for-senate/88738 |access-date=October 27, 2014}}</ref>

One-term incumbent Democrat [[Tom Udall]] had been elected with 61% of the vote in 2008. Former [[Doña Ana County, New Mexico|Doña Ana County]] Republican Party Chairman David Clements and former New Mexico Republican Party Chairman [[Allen Weh]] sought the Republican nomination.<ref>[http://www.rollcall.com/news/dems_mission_in_new_mexico_breaking_steve_pearce-229057-1.html Democrats' Mission in N.M.: Breaking Steve Pearce], rollcall.com; accessed November 7, 2014.</ref> Weh won the June 3 primary but lost to Udall in the general election.

{{Main|2014 United States Senate election in North Carolina}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from North Carolina}}

One-term incumbent Democrat [[Kay Hagan]] had been elected with 53% of the vote against incumbent Republican [[Elizabeth Dole]] in 2008. Hagan was seeking re-election.<ref>{{Cite news |last=Sullivan |first=Sean |title=Sen. Kay Hagan says she will run for re-election in 2014 |newspaper=[[The Washington Post]] |publisher=washingtonpost.com |url=https://www.washingtonpost.com/blogs/the-fix/wp/2012/11/27/sen-kay-hagan-says-she-will-run-for-re-election-in-2014 |access-date=February 8, 2013}} {{Dead link | date= August 2015 | bot= RjwilmsiBot}}</ref><ref name="Hoffman">{{Cite web |title=Results from Tuesday's primary elections |date=May 7, 2014 |url=http://www.news-record.com/news/government/elections/article_23868bc8-d4ed-11e3-aa03-0017a43b2370.html |access-date=November 7, 2014}}</ref><ref name="Hoffman2">{{Cite web |last=Hoffman |first=H. Scott |date=May 7, 2014 |title=2014 general election results |url=http://www.news-record.com/news/government/elections/article_23868bc8-d4ed-11e3-aa03-0017a43b2370.html |access-date=May 7, 2014 |publisher=news-record.com}}</ref>

State House Speaker [[Thom Tillis]] was the Republican nominee.<ref>{{Cite web |title=Thom Tillis beats back Tea Party challenger in North Carolina | date=May 6, 2014 |url=https://www.politico.com/story/2014/05/primary-results-north-carolina-ohio-indiana-106416.html?hp=l1 |access-date=November 7, 2014 |publisher=Politico.com}}</ref> [[Sean Haugh]] won the Libertarian nomination.<ref name="Hoffman" />

== Oklahoma ==
{{See also|List of United States senators from Oklahoma|2014 United States House of Representatives elections in Oklahoma}}
There were 2 elections in Oklahoma, due to the resignation of [[Tom Coburn]].

=== Oklahoma (special) ===
{{Infobox election
 | election_name      = Oklahoma special election
 | country            = Oklahoma
 | type               = presidential
 | ongoing            = no
 | previous_election  = 2010 United States Senate election in Oklahoma
 | previous_year      = 2010
 | next_election      = 2016 United States Senate election in Oklahoma
 | next_year          = 2016
 | image_size         = 125x136px
 | image1             = James Lankford, Official Portrait, 112th Congress.jpg
 | nominee1           = '''[[James Lankford]]'''
 | party1             = Republican Party (US)
 | popular_vote1      = '''557,002'''
 | percentage1        = '''67.9%'''
 | image2             = Johnson c.jpg
 | nominee2           = [[Constance N. Johnson|Connie Johnson]]
 | party2             = Democratic Party (US)
 | popular_vote2      = 237,923
 | percentage2        = 29.0%
 | map_image          = 2014 United States Senate special election in Oklahoma results map by county.svg
 | map_size           = 300px
| map_caption = Country results <br/>
 '''Lankford:''' {{legend0|#e27f7f|50–60%}} {{legend0|#d75d5d|60–70%}} {{legend0|#d72f30|70–80%}} {{legend0|#c21b18|80–90%}}
 | title              = U.S. Senator
 | before_election    = [[Tom Coburn]]
 | before_party       = Republican Party (US)
 | after_election     = [[James Lankford]]
 | after_party        = Republican Party (US)
}}
{{Main|2014 United States Senate special election in Oklahoma}}

Two-term incumbent Republican [[Tom Coburn]] had been re-elected with 71% of the vote in 2010, and was not scheduled to be up for election again until 2016. However, Coburn announced his intention to resign at the end of the [[113th United States Congress|113th Congress]]. A special election to fill his seat took place in November 2014, concurrent with the other Senate elections.<ref name="OkSpecial">{{Cite news |last=Cahn |first=Emily |date=January 17, 2014 |title=Special Election Dates Set to Replace Coburn |work=Roll Call |url=http://atr.rollcall.com/oklahoma-gov-sets-dates-for-election-to-replace-coburn |access-date=January 17, 2014 |archive-date=January 19, 2014 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20140119043945/http://atr.rollcall.com/oklahoma-gov-sets-dates-for-election-to-replace-coburn/ |url-status=dead }}</ref> Congressman [[James Lankford]] was the Republican nominee.<ref name="OkCan">{{Cite news |last=Taylor |first=Jessica |date=January 17, 2014 |title=Crowded primary likely in race for Coburn seat |work=The Hill |url=https://thehill.com/blogs/ballot-box/senate-races/195783-crowded-primary-likely-in-race-for-coburn-seat/ |access-date=January 17, 2014}}</ref><ref name="Oklahoma Farm Team">{{Cite news |last=Cahn |first=Emily |date=January 15, 2014 |title=In Oklahoma, Open Seats Could Come Sooner |work=Roll Call |url=http://www.rollcall.com/news/in_oklahoma_open_seats_could_come_sooner-230200-1.html?pg=2 |access-date=January 15, 2014}}</ref> State Senator [[Constance N. Johnson|Connie Johnson]] was the Democratic nominee.<ref>{{Cite news |last=Felder |first=Ben |date=April 8, 2014 |title=Johnson announces Democratic bid for U.S. Senate |work=Oklahoma Gazette |url=http://www.okgazette.com/oklahoma/article-20960-johnson-announces-de.html |url-status=dead |access-date=May 3, 2014 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20141017005601/http://okgazette.com/oklahoma/article-20960-johnson-announces-de.html |archive-date=October 17, 2014 |df=mdy-all}}</ref>

{{Main|2014 United States Senate election in Oregon}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Oregon}}

One-term incumbent Democrat [[Jeff Merkley]] was narrowly elected with 49% of the vote in 2008. Merkley was running for a second term. State representative [[Jason Conger]], attorney Tim Crawley, IT consultant Mark Callahan, neurosurgeon Dr. [[Monica Wehby]], and former [[Linn County, Oregon|Linn County]] Republican Chair [[Jo Rae Perkins]] all ran for the Republican nomination,<ref name="OPB">{{Cite news |date=January 27, 2014 |title=The Republican Challengers To Merkley And Kitzhaber |work=OPB |url=http://www.opb.org/radio/programs/thinkoutloud/segment/who-are-the-republican-challengers-to-merkley-and-kitzhaber |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20140408232302/http://www.opb.org/radio/programs/thinkoutloud/segment/who-are-the-republican-challengers-to-merkley-and-kitzhaber/ |url-status=dead |archive-date=April 8, 2014 |access-date=April 7, 2014 }}</ref> with Wehby ultimately winning the nomination in the May 20 primary.<ref name="yahoo1">{{Cite web |date=May 21, 2014 |title=Is the party over? Tea Party shut out in Tuesday's primaries |url=https://news.yahoo.com/tea-party-shut-out-135533528.html |access-date=June 27, 2014 |publisher=News.yahoo.com}}</ref>

== South Carolina ==
{{See also|List of United States senators from South Carolina|2014 United States House of Representatives elections in South Carolina}}
There were 2 elections in South Carolina, due to the resignation of [[Jim DeMint]].

=== South Carolina (regular) ===
{{Infobox election

 | country            = South Carolina

'''Graham:''' {{legend0|#ffb2b2|40–50%}} {{legend0|#e27f7f|50–60%}} {{legend0|#d75d5d|60–70%}} {{legend0|#d72f30|70–80%}}
'''Hutto:''' {{legend0|#a5b0ff|40–50%}} {{legend0|#7996e2|50–60%}} {{legend0|#6674de|60–70%}} {{legend0|#584cde|70–80%}}

 | before_election    = [[Lindsey Graham]]
 | before_party       = Republican Party (US)
 | after_election     = [[Lindsey Graham]]
 | after_party        = Republican Party (US)

{{Main|2014 United States Senate election in South Carolina}}

Two-term Republican [[Lindsey Graham]] had been re-elected with 58% of the vote in 2008. Graham won the Republican nomination over a field that included state senator [[Lee Bright]]. State Senator [[Brad Hutto]] won the Democratic nomination.<ref>{{Cite web |date=June 10, 2014 |title=Brad Hutto wins Democratic nomination for US Senate seat |url=http://www.live5news.com/story/25732329/democrats-face-off-in-primary-for-us-senate-seat |access-date=November 7, 2014 |publisher=WCSC}}</ref>

=== South Carolina (special) ===
{{Infobox election
 | election_name      = South Carolina special election
 | country            = South Carolina
 | type               = presidential
 | ongoing            = no
 | previous_election  = 2010 United States Senate election in South Carolina
 | previous_year      = 2010
 | next_election      = 2016 United States Senate election in South Carolina
 | next_year          = 2016
 | image_size         = 125x136px
 | image1             = Tim Scott, official portrait, 113th Congress (cropped).jpg
 | nominee1           = '''[[Tim Scott]]'''
 | party1             = Republican Party (US)
 | popular_vote1      = '''757,215'''
 | percentage1        = '''61.1%'''
 | image2             = Joyce Dickerson.jpg
 | nominee2           = [[Joyce Dickerson]]
 | party2             = Democratic Party (US)
