 | percentage2        = 37.1%
 | map_image          = 2014 United States Senate special election in South Carolina results map by county.svg
 | map_size           = 220px
| map_caption = County results<br />'''Scott:''' {{legend0|#ffb2b2|40–50%}} {{legend0|#e27f7f|50–60%}} {{legend0|#d75d5d|60–70%}} {{legend0|#d72f30|70–80%}} {{legend0|#c21b18|80–90%}}<br />'''Dickerson:''' {{legend0|#7996e2|50–60%}} {{legend0|#6674de|60–70%}} {{legend0|#584cde|70–80%}}

 | before_party       = Republican Party (US)

{{Main|2014 United States Senate special election in South Carolina}}

[[Jim DeMint]] had been elected to a second term in 2010, but resigned from the Senate in January 2013 to become president of [[The Heritage Foundation]], a conservative think-tank. Governor [[Nikki Haley]] appointed [[United States House of Representatives|Congressman]] [[Tim Scott]] as DeMint's replacement.<ref>{{Cite news |date=December 17, 2012 |title=Tim Scott to succeed Jim DeMint in Senate |work=[[Politico]] |publisher=Politico.com |url=https://www.politico.com/story/2012/12/tim-scott-to-succeed-demint-in-senate-85169.html?hp=f1 |access-date=December 17, 2012}}</ref> Scott, an African-American, was the Republican nominee to serve out the remainder of DeMint's term. Scott is the first African-American Republican since shortly after Reconstruction to represent a Southern state. [[Richland County, South Carolina|Richland County]] Councilwoman [[Joyce Dickerson]] won the Democratic nomination.<ref>{{Cite news |date=June 10, 2014 |title=Dickerson wins Dem primary, to face Scott in Nov. |publisher=wistv.com |url=http://www.wistv.com/story/25745002/dickerson-wins-dem-primary-to-face-scott-in-nov |access-date=June 10, 2014}}</ref>

== South Dakota ==

{{Main|2014 United States Senate election in South Dakota}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from South Dakota}}

Three-term incumbent Democrat [[Tim Johnson (U.S. Senator)|Tim Johnson]] had been re-elected with 63% of the vote in 2008. Johnson announced on March 26, 2013, that he would not run for re-election.<ref>{{Cite news |last=Camia |first=Catalina |date=March 25, 2013 |title=Report: Democratic Sen. Johnson to retire |publisher=[[USA Today]] |url=https://www.usatoday.com/story/news/politics/2013/03/25/tim-johnson-senate-retire-south-dakota/2018537 |access-date=March 25, 2013}}</ref> Former Congressional aide [[Rick Weiland]] was the Democratic nominee.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Jaffe |first=Alexandra |date=May 12, 2013 |title=South Dakota Dems' primary fears ease in race to replace Sen. Johnson |url=https://thehill.com/blogs/ballot-box/senate-races/150253-south-dakota-dems-primary-fears-ease-in-race-to-replace-sen-johnson/ |access-date=November 7, 2014 |work=The Hill}}</ref>

Among Republicans, former two-term governor [[Mike Rounds]] announced his candidacy for the GOP nomination on November 29, 2012.<ref>{{Cite web |date=November 29, 2012 |title=Mike Rounds announces 2014 U.S. Senate candidacy |url=http://www.mitchellrepublic.com/event/article/id/73011 |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://archive.today/20130629084730/http://www.mitchellrepublic.com/event/article/id/73011 |archive-date=June 29, 2013 |access-date=November 29, 2012 |publisher=The Daily Republic |df=mdy-all}}</ref> Rounds won the Republican nomination over state senator [[Larry Rhoden]], state representative [[Stace Nelson]], and physician Annette Bosworth.<ref>{{Cite news |date=August 13, 2013 |title=Stace Nelson to announce run for U.S. Senate |work=Argus Leader |url=http://www.argusleader.com/article/20130813/UPDATES/130813001/Stace-Nelson-announce-run-U-S-Senate?gcheck=1 |url-status=dead |access-date=August 16, 2013 |archive-url=https://archive.today/20130816134317/http://www.argusleader.com/article/20130813/UPDATES/130813001/Stace-Nelson-announce-run-U-S-Senate?gcheck=1 |archive-date=August 16, 2013}}</ref>

Former Republican U.S. Senator [[Larry Pressler]] and Republican [[South Dakota Senate|State Senator]] [[Gordon Howie]] ran as [[independent (politics)|independents]].<ref>{{Cite web |last=Cost |first=Jay |date=June 27, 2014 |title=Former South Dakota Sen. Larry Pressler running as independent |url=http://washingtonexaminer.com |access-date=November 7, 2014 |publisher=Washington Examiner}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web |date=April 4, 2014 |title=Ensuring a Choice for South Dakota Conservatives |url=http://www.americanclarion.com/ensuring-choice-conservatives-29803 |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20140407080904/http://www.americanclarion.com/ensuring-choice-conservatives-29803 |archive-date=April 7, 2014 |publisher=Americanclarion.com |df=mdy-all}}</ref> Pressler did not commit to caucusing with either party, while Howie said he would caucus with the Senate Republicans.<ref>{{Cite news |last=Sullivan |first=Sean |date=October 10, 2014 |title=Meet Larry Presler, the one man band shaking up the battle for the Senate majority |newspaper=The Washington Post |url=https://www.washingtonpost.com/blogs/post-politics/wp/2014/10/10/meet-larry-pressler-the-one-man-band-shaking-up-the-battle-for-the-senate-majority |access-date=October 11, 2014}}</ref><ref>{{Cite news |title=Five questions with Senate candidate Gordon Howie |url=http://rapidcityjournal.com/news/local/five-questions-with-senate-candidate-gordon-howie/article_dbd51854-2494-55be-b5bd-fed6eeb509a2.html |access-date=October 11, 2014}}</ref>

== Tennessee ==

{{See also|List of United States senators from Tennessee}}

Two-term incumbent Republican [[Lamar Alexander]] had been re-elected with 65% of the vote in 2008. Alexander sought re-election to a third term.<ref name="alexander">{{Cite web |date=September 20, 2011 |title=Alexander quitting leadership post in Senate |url=https://www.politico.com/news/stories/0911/63919_Page2.html |website=Politico |publisher=Politico.com}}</ref> On August 7, 2014, Alexander won the Republican nomination over six challengers, including State Representative [[Joe S. Carr|Joe Carr]].<ref>{{Cite web |last=ABC News |title=Tennessee Sen. Lamar Alexander Defeats Tea Party Challenger Joe Carr |url=https://abcnews.go.com/blogs/politics/2014/08/tennessee-sen-lamar-alexander-defeats-tea-party-challenger-joe-carr |access-date=October 27, 2014 |website=ABC News Blogs}}</ref>

On November 4, 2014, Alexander faced Democratic nominee Gordon Ball, [[Libertarian Party (United States)|Libertarian Party]] nominee Joshua James,<ref name="tennessee.gov">{{Cite web|url=http://www.tennessee.gov/sos/election/PetitionsFiled.pdf|title=Petitions Filed for Governor, United States Senate, and United States House of Representatives|accessdate=May 7, 2023|archive-date=September 11, 2014|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20140911235753/http://www.tennessee.gov/sos/election/PetitionsFiled.pdf|url-status=dead}}</ref> [[Constitution Party (United States)|Constitution Party]] nominee Joe Wilmothm, and independent Danny Page<ref name="tennessee.gov" /> also ran in the general election.

== Texas ==
{{Infobox election
 | election_name      = Texas election
 | country            = Texas
 | type               = presidential
 | ongoing            = no
 | previous_election  = 2008 United States Senate election in Texas
 | previous_year      = 2008
 | next_election      = 2020 United States Senate election in Texas
 | next_year          = 2020
 | image_size         = 125x136px

 | image1             = John Cornyn official senate portrait.jpg
 | nominee1           = '''[[John Cornyn]]'''
 | party1             = Republican Party (US)
 | popular_vote1      = '''2,861,531'''
 | percentage1        = '''61.56%'''

 | image2             = No image.svg
 | nominee2           = David Alameel
 | party2             = Democratic Party (US)
 | popular_vote2      = 1,597,387
 | percentage2        = 34.36%

 | map_image          = 2014 United States Senate election in Texas results map by county.svg
 | map_size           = 300px
| map_caption = '''Cornyn:''' {{legend0|#ffb2b2|40–50%}} {{legend0|#e27f7f|50–60%}} {{legend0|#d75d5d|60–70%}} {{legend0|#d72f30|70–80%}} {{legend0|#c21b18|80–90%}} {{legend0|#a80000|>90%}}<br>'''Alameel:''' {{legend0|#a5b0ff|40–50%}} {{legend0|#7996e2|50–60%}} {{legend0|#6674de|60–70%}} {{legend0|#584cde|70–80%}}

 | title              = U.S. Senator
 | before_election    = [[John Cornyn]]
 | before_party       = Republican Party (US)
 | after_election     = [[John Cornyn]]
 | after_party        = Republican Party (US)
}}

{{Main|2014 United States Senate election in Texas}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Texas}}

Two-term incumbent Republican [[John Cornyn]], the [[Senate Minority Whip]], had been re-elected with 55% of the vote in 2008. Cornyn sought re-election, and won the 2014 Republican primary with 59% of the vote. [[David Alameel]], a dentist, and Kesha Rogers, a volunteer for [[Lyndon LaRouche|The Lyndon LaRouche Policy Institute]], faced each other in a run-off election for the Democratic nomination.<ref name="TXDem">{{Cite news |last=Gillman |first=Todd J. |date=December 2, 2013 |title=Dallas dentist David Alameel brings fortune to U.S. Senate race |work=The Dallas Morning News |url=http://www.dallasnews.com/news/politics/headlines/20131202-dallas-dentist-david-alameel-brings-big-fortune-to-u.s.-senate-race.ece |access-date=December 10, 2013}}</ref> Alameel won the run-off and was the Democratic nominee.<ref>{{Cite web |date=May 27, 2014 |title=Texas Democrats nominate Alameel for US Senate |url=http://www.khou.com/news/Texas-Democrats-nominate-Alameel-for-US-Senate--260857831.html |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://archive.today/20140619144133/http://www.khou.com/news/Texas-Democrats-nominate-Alameel-for-US-Senate--260857831.html |archive-date=June 19, 2014 |access-date=June 27, 2014 |publisher=Khou.com |df=mdy-all}}</ref>

One-term incumbent Democrat [[Mark Warner]] had been elected with 65% of the vote in 2008; he sought re-election. [[Ed Gillespie]], former [[Republican National Committee|RNC]] Chairman and presidential adviser, ran for the Republican nomination. [[Robert Sarvis]], the Libertarian nominee for [[Governor of Virginia|Governor]] in [[2013 Virginia gubernatorial election|2013]], also ran.<ref name="auto" />

== West Virginia ==
{{Infobox election
 | election_name      = West Virginia election
 | country            = West Virginia
 | type               = presidential
 | ongoing            = no
 | previous_election  = 2008 United States Senate election in West Virginia
 | previous_year      = 2008
 | next_election      = 2020 United States Senate election in West Virginia
 | next_year          = 2020
 | image_size         = 125x136px

 | image1             = Shelley moore capito (cropped).jpg
 | nominee1           = '''[[Shelley Moore Capito]]'''
 | party1             = Republican Party (US)
 | popular_vote1      = '''280,123'''
 | percentage1        = '''62.1%'''

 | image2             = NatalieTennant7 (cropped).jpg
 | nominee2           = [[Natalie Tennant]]
 | party2             = Democratic Party (US)
 | popular_vote2      = 155,456
 | percentage2        = 34.5%

 | map_image          = 2014 United States Senate election in West Virginia results map by county.svg
 | map_size           = 250px
| map_caption = '''Moore Capito:''' {{legend0|#e27f7f|50–60%}} {{legend0|#d75d5d|60–70%}} {{legend0|#d72f30|70–80%}} {{legend0|#c21b18|80–90%}}

 | title              = U.S. Senator
 | before_election    = [[Jay Rockefeller]]
 | before_party       = Democratic Party (US)
 | after_election     = [[Shelley Moore Capito]]
 | after_party        = Republican Party (US)
}}

{{Main|2014 United States Senate election in West Virginia}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from West Virginia}}

Five-term incumbent Democrat [[Jay Rockefeller]] had been re-elected with 64% of the vote in 2008. He announced on January 11, 2013, that he would not seek re-election to a sixth term. Secretary of State [[Natalie Tennant]] won the Democratic nomination.<ref name="Tennant Running">{{Cite news |last=Livingston |first=Abby |date=September 13, 2013 |title=Democrats Land Recruit for West Virginia Senate Race |work=Roll Call |url=http://atr.rollcall.com/democrats-land-recruit-for-west-virginia-senate-race |access-date=September 14, 2013 |archive-date=September 16, 2013 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20130916010547/http://atr.rollcall.com/democrats-land-recruit-for-west-virginia-senate-race/ |url-status=dead }}</ref>

On November 26, 2012, Republican Congresswoman [[Shelley Moore Capito]] announced her plans to run for the seat, in hopes of becoming the first Republican senator elected from West Virginia since 1956.<ref>{{Cite web |title=Shelley Moore Capito makes Senate bid vs. Jay Rockefeller official |url=https://www.politico.com/story/2012/11/capito-makes-senate-bid-official-084211?hp=f3 |access-date=December 3, 2012 |website=Politico | date=November 25, 2012 |publisher=Politico.com}}</ref> Moore Capito won the Republican nomination and the general election, the first woman to serve as United States Senator from West Virginia.

== Wyoming ==

{{Main|2014 United States Senate election in Wyoming}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Wyoming}}

Three-term incumbent Republican [[Mike Enzi]] had been re-elected with 76% of the vote in 2008. Enzi sought re-election. [[Liz Cheney]], daughter of former Vice President [[Dick Cheney]], briefly entered the race for the Republican nomination, but dropped her bid in January 2014.<ref name="theguardian">{{Cite web |date=January 6, 2014 |title=Liz Cheney ends Senate campaign over 'serious health issues' in family |url=https://www.theguardian.com/world/2014/jan/06/liz-cheney-ends-senate-campaign-health-family |access-date=January 9, 2014 |work=The Guardian}}</ref> On August 19, Enzi won the Republican primary election with 82% of the vote, and Democrat Charlie Hardy, a former Catholic priest, won his party's primary election with 48% of the vote.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Staff/Associated Press |date=June 3, 2013 |title=Wyoming - Summary Vote Results |url=http://hosted.ap.org/dynamic/files/elections/2014/by_state/WY_US_Senate_0819.html?SITE=AP&SECTION=POLITICS |publisher=hosted.ap.org}}</ref>
